What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been worrying more than I’d like. I try to be sensible about things—I make my lists, check the dates, make sure I’ve done what I can—but then my mind just keeps going anyway. I can’t always seem to turn it off once it starts, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted thoughts that keep popping into your mind even when you try not to think them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not in a strange way, I don’t think—more like once a worry gets started, it just keeps coming back even when I’ve told myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can.” I try to be reasonable about it, but my mind doesn’t always seem to accept that and move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries keep coming back, do you feel driven to do something over and over—like checking, counting, or repeating things—to feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. Not only that, no. I mean, that’s part of it, of course, but my mind doesn’t seem to stay neatly on one thing.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been worrying more than I’d like. I try to be sensible about things—I make my lists, check the dates, make sure I’ve done what I can—but then my mind just keeps going anyway. I can’t always seem to turn it off once it starts, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted thoughts that keep popping into your mind even when you try not to think them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not in a strange way, I don’t think—more like once a worry gets started, it just keeps coming back even when I’ve told myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can.” I try to be reasonable about it, but my mind doesn’t always seem to accept that and move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries keep coming back, do you feel driven to do something over and over—like checking, counting, or repeating things—to feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might look at something again if I’m unsure, like anyone would, but I don’t feel like I have to count things or repeat something over and over to make the worry go away. It’s more that the thought just… lingers, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it did start around a change, yes. It’s been about seven months now… shortly after a friend of mine died. We had some projects we’d been trying to keep moving, and I think I sort of tried to carry on with them and be useful. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when I noticed the worrying becoming harder to put down.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries about many different parts of your life, or mostly about matters connected to your friend's death and what happened afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s… not only about my friend, no. That may have set it off, or made it worse, but now it sort of moves around. I’ll worry about the exhibition schedule, and then my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nI know those aren’t all catastrophes, really, but in my head small things can start to feel like they might turn into something much bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I try to be sensible about it—I’ll make a list, check the dates, tell myself I’ve done what I can—but even then my mind doesn’t quite let it go.\n\nIt’s not that I want to sit there worrying. I just can’t always seem to turn it off once it gets started, if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you often felt restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. Not every minute of every day, of course, but if I’m being honest, it’s been about seven months now—since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep our projects going.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down once things were more organized, but it hasn’t really gone away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I try to be sensible about it—I’ll make a list, check the dates, tell myself I’ve done what I can—but even then my mind doesn’t quite let it go.\n\nIt’s not that I want to sit there worrying. I just can’t always seem to turn it off once it gets started, if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you often felt restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I do feel sort of wound up a lot of the time, even just sitting in my living room.\n\nI’ll catch myself tapping my fingers during phone calls, or getting up to straighten something that doesn’t really need straightening, just because sitting still feels… jumpy, I suppose. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this kind of worry on most days for the past six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has been most days. Not every minute of every day, of course, but if I’m being honest, it’s been about seven months now—since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep our projects going.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down once things were more organized, but it hasn’t really gone away. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting tired more easily than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit more than I’d like, I suppose. Even if I sleep through the night, I sometimes wake up feeling like I’ve already done half a day’s worth of things.\n\nIt’s not that I can’t manage, but after something ordinary—watering the garden beds, or sorting papers for book club—I often need to sit down for a while and sort of gather myself. Is that what you mean by getting tired more easily?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything very clear that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll reread a paragraph or lose my place, but I’m not sure if that’s just normal distraction. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of concentrating you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you often felt physically tense in your muscles?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t call it severe, but by the evening my shoulders and neck can feel very tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced all day.\n\nI’ve even caught myself clenching my jaw when I’m just reading or planning things for the garden center activities. I suppose that counts as muscle tension, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worrying is mainly about your friend’s death and the responsibilities that came after it?\n\nPatient: Not only that, no. I mean, that’s part of it, of course, but my mind doesn’t seem to stay neatly on one thing.\n\nIt wanders from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans, then to whether the gutters on the house need fixing… and then somehow those little things start feeling as though they could turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them.\n\nI know that may sound a bit silly—I’m probably making more of it than I should—but it does seem to spread around. Is that helpful to say?\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do still keep up with the garden and the book club.\n\nBut I’ve noticed I’m not as prepared as I used to be, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it, because it just feels harder to manage than it should. I suppose that is interfering somewhat, isn’t it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I can’t always seem to turn it off once it starts, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted thoughts that keep popping into your mind even when you try not to think them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I’ll worry about the exhibition schedule, and then my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nI know those aren’t all catastrophes, really, but in my head small things can start to feel like they might turn into something much bigger. I mean, that’s part of it, of course, but my mind doesn’t seem to stay neatly on one thing.\n\nIt wanders from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans, then to whether the gutters on the house need fixing… and then somehow those little things start feeling as though they could turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them.\n\nI know that may sound a bit silly—I’m probably making more of it than I should—but it does seem to spread around. Is that helpful to say?\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when I noticed the worrying becoming harder to put down.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries about many different parts of your life, or mostly about matters connected to your friend's death and what happened afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s… not only about my friend, no. That may have set it off, or made it worse, but now it sort of moves around. I’ll worry about the exhibition schedule, and then my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nI know those aren’t all catastrophes, really, but in my head small things can start to feel like they might turn into something much bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I try to be sensible about it—I’ll make a list, check the dates, tell myself I’ve done what I can—but even then my mind doesn’t quite let it go.\n\nIt’s not that I want to sit there worrying. I just can’t always seem to turn it off once it gets started, if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you often felt restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I do feel sort of wound up a lot of the time, even just sitting in my living room.\n\nI’ll catch myself tapping my fingers during phone calls, or getting up to straighten something that doesn’t really need straightening, just because sitting still feels… jumpy, I suppose. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this kind of worry on most days for the past six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has been most days. Not every minute of every day, of course, but if I’m being honest, it’s been about seven months now—since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep our projects going.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down once things were more organized, but it hasn’t really gone away. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting tired more easily than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit more than I’d like, I suppose. Even if I sleep through the night, I sometimes wake up feeling like I’ve already done half a day’s worth of things.\n\nIt’s not that I can’t manage, but after something ordinary—watering the garden beds, or sorting papers for book club—I often need to sit down for a while and sort of gather myself. Is that what you mean by getting tired more easily?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything very clear that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll reread a paragraph or lose my place, but I’m not sure if that’s just normal distraction. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of concentrating you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you often felt physically tense in your muscles?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t call it severe, but by the evening my shoulders and neck can feel very tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced all day.\n\nI’ve even caught myself clenching my jaw when I’m just reading or planning things for the garden center activities. I suppose that counts as muscle tension, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worrying is mainly about your friend’s death and the responsibilities that came after it?\n\nPatient: Not only that, no. I mean, that’s part of it, of course, but my mind doesn’t seem to stay neatly on one thing.\n\nIt wanders from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans, then to whether the gutters on the house need fixing… and then somehow those little things start feeling as though they could turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them.\n\nI know that may sound a bit silly—I’m probably making more of it than I should—but it does seem to spread around. Is that helpful to say?\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do still keep up with the garden and the book club.\n\nBut I’ve noticed I’m not as prepared as I used to be, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it, because it just feels harder to manage than it should. I suppose that is interfering somewhat, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is more than the situation really calls for, though I don’t always see it at first.\n\nIt’s like my mind keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter’s travel plans, to whether the gutters need fixing—and then those small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep watch over them.\n\nI know that may be me overdoing it a bit… but yes, I think it does get out of proportion.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it real trouble sleeping. I mean, I do get to bed, and most nights I manage. Sometimes I lie there a bit longer than I’d like, thinking, but I don’t know if that counts as a sleep problem exactly.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of concentrating you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you often felt physically tense in your muscles?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t call it severe, but by the evening my shoulders and neck can feel very tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced all day.\n\nI’ve even caught myself clenching my jaw when I’m just reading or planning things for the garden center activities. I suppose that counts as muscle tension, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worrying is mainly about your friend’s death and the responsibilities that came after it?\n\nPatient: Not only that, no. I mean, that’s part of it, of course, but my mind doesn’t seem to stay neatly on one thing.\n\nIt wanders from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans, then to whether the gutters on the house need fixing… and then somehow those little things start feeling as though they could turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them.\n\nI know that may sound a bit silly—I’m probably making more of it than I should—but it does seem to spread around. Is that helpful to say?\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do still keep up with the garden and the book club.\n\nBut I’ve noticed I’m not as prepared as I used to be, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it, because it just feels harder to manage than it should. I suppose that is interfering somewhat, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is more than the situation really calls for, though I don’t always see it at first.\n\nIt’s like my mind keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter’s travel plans, to whether the gutters need fixing—and then those small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep watch over them.\n\nI know that may be me overdoing it a bit… but yes, I think it does get out of proportion.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it real trouble sleeping. I mean, I do get to bed, and most nights I manage. Sometimes I lie there a bit longer than I’d like, thinking, but I don’t know if that counts as a sleep problem exactly.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is mostly part of grieving your friend?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… at least that’s what I’ve been telling myself. It did seem to start after my friend died, maybe shortly after, when I was trying to keep our projects moving.\n\nBut it’s been about seven months now, so I suppose I’m not sure if it’s still just grief, or if it’s become its own sort of pattern. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been worrying more than I’d like. I try to be sensible about things—I make my lists, check the dates, make sure I’ve done what I can—but then my mind just keeps going anyway. I can’t always seem to turn it off once it starts, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted thoughts that keep popping into your mind even when you try not to think them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not in a strange way, I don’t think—more like once a worry gets started, it just keeps coming back even when I’ve told myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can.” I try to be reasonable about it, but my mind doesn’t always seem to accept that and move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries keep coming back, do you feel driven to do something over and over—like checking, counting, or repeating things—to feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might look at something again if I’m unsure, like anyone would, but I don’t feel like I have to count things or repeat something over and over to make the worry go away. It’s more that the thought just… lingers, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it did start around a change, yes. It’s been about seven months now… shortly after a friend of mine died. We had some projects we’d been trying to keep moving, and I think I sort of tried to carry on with them and be useful. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when I noticed the worrying becoming harder to put down.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries about many different parts of your life, or mostly about matters connected to your friend's death and what happened afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s… not only about my friend, no. That may have set it off, or made it worse, but now it sort of moves around. I’ll worry about the exhibition schedule, and then my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nI know those aren’t all catastrophes, really, but in my head small things can start to feel like they might turn into something much bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I try to be sensible about it—I’ll make a list, check the dates, tell myself I’ve done what I can—but even then my mind doesn’t quite let it go.\n\nIt’s not that I want to sit there worrying. I just can’t always seem to turn it off once it gets started, if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you often felt restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I suppose that is interfering somewhat, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is more than the situation really calls for, though I don’t always see it at first.\n\nIt’s like my mind keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter’s travel plans, to whether the gutters need fixing—and then those small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep watch over them.\n\nI know that may be me overdoing it a bit… but yes, I think it does get out of proportion.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it real trouble sleeping. I mean, I do get to bed, and most nights I manage. Sometimes I lie there a bit longer than I’d like, thinking, but I don’t know if that counts as a sleep problem exactly.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is mostly part of grieving your friend?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… at least that’s what I’ve been telling myself. It did seem to start after my friend died, maybe shortly after, when I was trying to keep our projects moving.\n\nBut it’s been about seven months now, so I suppose I’m not sure if it’s still just grief, or if it’s become its own sort of pattern. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If your friend had not died, do you think this level of worrying would still be happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know for certain, of course… but I don’t think it would be quite like this.\n\nIt really seemed to begin about seven months ago, shortly after my friend died, when I was trying to keep our projects moving. So I suppose that’s why I connect the two. I’m not sure if that answers it properly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been worrying more than I’d like. I try to be sensible about things—I make my lists, check the dates, make sure I’ve done what I can—but then my mind just keeps going anyway. I can’t always seem to turn it off once it starts, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted thoughts that keep popping into your mind even when you try not to think them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not in a strange way, I don’t think—more like once a worry gets started, it just keeps coming back even when I’ve told myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can.” I try to be reasonable about it, but my mind doesn’t always seem to accept that and move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries keep coming back, do you feel driven to do something over and over—like checking, counting, or repeating things—to feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might look at something again if I’m unsure, like anyone would, but I don’t feel like I have to count things or repeat something over and over to make the worry go away. It’s more that the thought just… lingers, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it did start around a change, yes. It’s been about seven months now… shortly after a friend of mine died. We had some projects we’d been trying to keep moving, and I think I sort of tried to carry on with them and be useful. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when I noticed the worrying becoming harder to put down.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries about many different parts of your life, or mostly about matters connected to your friend's death and what happened afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s… not only about my friend, no. That may have set it off, or made it worse, but now it sort of moves around. I’ll worry about the exhibition schedule, and then my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nI know those aren’t all catastrophes, really, but in my head small things can start to feel like they might turn into something much bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I try to be sensible about it—I’ll make a list, check the dates, tell myself I’ve done what I can—but even then my mind doesn’t quite let it go.\n\nIt’s not that I want to sit there worrying. I just can’t always seem to turn it off once it gets started, if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you often felt restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I do feel sort of wound up a lot of the time, even just sitting in my living room.\n\nI’ll catch myself tapping my fingers during phone calls, or getting up to straighten something that doesn’t really need straightening, just because sitting still feels… jumpy, I suppose. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this kind of worry on most days for the past six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has been most days. Not every minute of every day, of course, but if I’m being honest, it’s been about seven months now—since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep our projects going.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down once things were more organized, but it hasn’t really gone away. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting tired more easily than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit more than I’d like, I suppose. Even if I sleep through the night, I sometimes wake up feeling like I’ve already done half a day’s worth of things.\n\nIt’s not that I can’t manage, but after something ordinary—watering the garden beds, or sorting papers for book club—I often need to sit down for a while and sort of gather myself. Is that what you mean by getting tired more easily?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed anything very clear that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll reread a paragraph or lose my place, but I’m not sure if that’s just normal distraction. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of concentrating you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you often felt physically tense in your muscles?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t call it severe, but by the evening my shoulders and neck can feel very tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced all day.\n\nI’ve even caught myself clenching my jaw when I’m just reading or planning things for the garden center activities. I suppose that counts as muscle tension, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worrying is mainly about your friend’s death and the responsibilities that came after it?\n\nPatient: Not only that, no. I mean, that’s part of it, of course, but my mind doesn’t seem to stay neatly on one thing.\n\nIt wanders from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans, then to whether the gutters on the house need fixing… and then somehow those little things start feeling as though they could turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them.\n\nI know that may sound a bit silly—I’m probably making more of it than I should—but it does seem to spread around. Is that helpful to say?\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do still keep up with the garden and the book club.\n\nBut I’ve noticed I’m not as prepared as I used to be, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it, because it just feels harder to manage than it should. I suppose that is interfering somewhat, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is more than the situation really calls for, though I don’t always see it at first.\n\nIt’s like my mind keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter’s travel plans, to whether the gutters need fixing—and then those small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep watch over them.\n\nI know that may be me overdoing it a bit… but yes, I think it does get out of proportion.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it real trouble sleeping. I mean, I do get to bed, and most nights I manage. Sometimes I lie there a bit longer than I’d like, thinking, but I don’t know if that counts as a sleep problem exactly.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is mostly part of grieving your friend?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… at least that’s what I’ve been telling myself. It did seem to start after my friend died, maybe shortly after, when I was trying to keep our projects moving.\n\nBut it’s been about seven months now, so I suppose I’m not sure if it’s still just grief, or if it’s become its own sort of pattern. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If your friend had not died, do you think this level of worrying would still be happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know for certain, of course… but I don’t think it would be quite like this.\n\nIt really seemed to begin about seven months ago, shortly after my friend died, when I was trying to keep our projects moving. So I suppose that’s why I connect the two. I’m not sure if that answers it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is out of proportion to what would normally be expected after your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, maybe a little out of proportion, though I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nIt’s not only about my friend anymore. My mind sort of wanders from one thing to the next—the exhibition schedule, my daughter’s travel plans, whether the gutters need fixing—and then these small things start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep track of them. Sometimes I lie there a bit longer than I’d like, thinking, but I don’t know if that counts as a sleep problem exactly.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is mostly part of grieving your friend?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… at least that’s what I’ve been telling myself. It did seem to start after my friend died, maybe shortly after, when I was trying to keep our projects moving.\n\nBut it’s been about seven months now, so I suppose I’m not sure if it’s still just grief, or if it’s become its own sort of pattern. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If your friend had not died, do you think this level of worrying would still be happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know for certain, of course… but I don’t think it would be quite like this.\n\nIt really seemed to begin about seven months ago, shortly after my friend died, when I was trying to keep our projects moving. So I suppose that’s why I connect the two. I’m not sure if that answers it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is out of proportion to what would normally be expected after your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, maybe a little out of proportion, though I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nIt’s not only about my friend anymore. My mind sort of wanders from one thing to the next—the exhibition schedule, my daughter’s travel plans, whether the gutters need fixing—and then these small things start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep track of them. I try to be sensible about things—I make my lists, check the dates, make sure I’ve done what I can—but then my mind just keeps going anyway. I can’t always seem to turn it off once it starts, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted thoughts that keep popping into your mind even when you try not to think them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not in a strange way, I don’t think—more like once a worry gets started, it just keeps coming back even when I’ve told myself, “You’ve made the list, you checked the dates, you’ve done what you can.” I try to be reasonable about it, but my mind doesn’t always seem to accept that and move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries keep coming back, do you feel driven to do something over and over—like checking, counting, or repeating things—to feel less anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might look at something again if I’m unsure, like anyone would, but I don’t feel like I have to count things or repeat something over and over to make the worry go away. It’s more that the thought just… lingers, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I suppose it did start around a change, yes. It’s been about seven months now… shortly after a friend of mine died. We had some projects we’d been trying to keep moving, and I think I sort of tried to carry on with them and be useful. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when I noticed the worrying becoming harder to put down.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries about many different parts of your life, or mostly about matters connected to your friend's death and what happened afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s… not only about my friend, no. That may have set it off, or made it worse, but now it sort of moves around. I’ll worry about the exhibition schedule, and then my daughter’s travel plans, and then something ordinary like whether the gutters need fixing.\n\nI know those aren’t all catastrophes, really, but in my head small things can start to feel like they might turn into something much bigger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I try to be sensible about it—I’ll make a list, check the dates, tell myself I’ve done what I can—but even then my mind doesn’t quite let it go.\n\nIt’s not that I want to sit there worrying. I just can’t always seem to turn it off once it gets started, if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you often felt restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I do feel sort of wound up a lot of the time, even just sitting in my living room.\n\nI’ll catch myself tapping my fingers during phone calls, or getting up to straighten something that doesn’t really need straightening, just because sitting still feels… jumpy, I suppose. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this kind of worry on most days for the past six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has been most days. Not every minute of every day, of course, but if I’m being honest, it’s been about seven months now—since shortly after my friend died and I was trying to keep our projects going.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down once things were more organized, but it hasn’t really gone away. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting tired more easily than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit more than I’d like, I suppose. Even if I sleep through the night, I sometimes wake up feeling like I’ve already done half a day’s worth of things.\n\nIt’s not that I can’t manage, but after something ordinary—watering the garden beds, or sorting papers for book club—I often need to sit down for a while and sort of gather myself. Is that what you mean by getting tired more easily?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Is that helpful to say?\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Is that the kind of concentrating you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you often felt physically tense in your muscles?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I wouldn’t call it severe, but by the evening my shoulders and neck can feel very tight, like I’ve been holding myself braced all day.\n\nI’ve even caught myself clenching my jaw when I’m just reading or planning things for the garden center activities. I suppose that counts as muscle tension, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your worrying is mainly about your friend’s death and the responsibilities that came after it?\n\nPatient: Not only that, no. I mean, that’s part of it, of course, but my mind doesn’t seem to stay neatly on one thing.\n\nIt wanders from the exhibition schedule to my daughter’s travel plans, then to whether the gutters on the house need fixing… and then somehow those little things start feeling as though they could turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep on top of them.\n\nI know that may sound a bit silly—I’m probably making more of it than I should—but it does seem to spread around. Is that helpful to say?\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I do still keep up with the garden and the book club.\n\nBut I’ve noticed I’m not as prepared as I used to be, and with the exhibition planning I’ve started turning down some parts of it, because it just feels harder to manage than it should. I suppose that is interfering somewhat, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it probably is more than the situation really calls for, though I don’t always see it at first.\n\nIt’s like my mind keeps roaming—from the exhibition schedule, to my daughter’s travel plans, to whether the gutters need fixing—and then those small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep watch over them.\n\nI know that may be me overdoing it a bit… but yes, I think it does get out of proportion.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it real trouble sleeping. I mean, I do get to bed, and most nights I manage. Sometimes I lie there a bit longer than I’d like, thinking, but I don’t know if that counts as a sleep problem exactly.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is mostly part of grieving your friend?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… at least that’s what I’ve been telling myself. It did seem to start after my friend died, maybe shortly after, when I was trying to keep our projects moving.\n\nBut it’s been about seven months now, so I suppose I’m not sure if it’s still just grief, or if it’s become its own sort of pattern. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If your friend had not died, do you think this level of worrying would still be happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know for certain, of course… but I don’t think it would be quite like this.\n\nIt really seemed to begin about seven months ago, shortly after my friend died, when I was trying to keep our projects moving. So I suppose that’s why I connect the two. I’m not sure if that answers it properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is out of proportion to what would normally be expected after your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, maybe a little out of proportion, though I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nIt’s not only about my friend anymore. My mind sort of wanders from one thing to the next—the exhibition schedule, my daughter’s travel plans, whether the gutters need fixing—and then these small things start feeling like they could turn into something much bigger if I don’t keep track of them. I know that may sound silly, but that’s how it feels sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this level of anxiety has lasted longer than you would expect as part of grieving your friend?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would say that. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been going on for about seven months now, really since shortly after my friend died, when I was trying to keep our projects moving. So… yes, it does feel longer than I would have expected, if that’s the right way to put it."
